Apple’s Music Hall signals a bigger strategic plan

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Why would Apple launch a concert venue?

This is the question I’ve been asking myself since it inaugurated its Apple Music Hall, nestled underneath its U.K. headquarters at London’s Battersea Power Station, in late September. Not just any performance space, this intimate 600-person, 48-speaker hall features Apple’s Spatial Audio technology, which uses Dolby Atmos and dynamic head tracking to turn flat stereo sound into a three-dimensional, 360-degree experience.

To my eye, the Music Hall is not just another listening room. It’s the start of an elaborate world-building exercise for Apple Music, designed to engage a community of hardcore fans. In doing so, Apple is looking to increase streaming subscriptions, convert artists to recording on its technology, and eventually challenge everyone from Google Home to Sonos for ownership of our in-home sound delivery systems.

An uphill battle

In just eight years since entering the TV market, Apple has shifted from startup streamer to a television mecca, delivering hit after hit including Severance, Slow Horses, Silo, Pluribus, Ted Lasso, and now Widow’s Bay. Astounding.

Meanwhile, in music it’s played second fiddle to Spotify, which not only boasts three times as many subscribers, but is first with innovative ideas like its Year End Review and the exclusive content serious listeners crave most. The Music Hall is Apple turning up the volume.

The risks here are real. While global spend on IRL brand experiences grew 8.3% to $138.9 billion in 2025 and is projected to rise a further 10.3% in 2026, the highways to hell have far outstripped the stairways to heaven. Samsung 837, the company’s former experiential space in New York City’s Meatpacking District, succeeded in attracting mothers with young children looking for rainy day distractions but few potential customers. Many car companies—Lexus, Genesis, Cadillac—have tried their hand at luxury lounges, only to be reminded that consumers are looking to them for, well, cars.


Those that are true to their roots, however, are hitting all the right notes. A24, the film and TV production company, experienced a huge brand win with its acquisition last year of the historic Cherry Lane Theater in NYC’s West Village. This has been a launchpad for new and experimental content that in turn has led to greater visibility for the studio, deeper fan engagement, and a two-year brand partnership with Dior. Porsche’s Experience Centers delight driving enthusiasts and attract new customers with authentic racing outings.

In seemingly sparing no expense to create a world-class performance space, Apple is signaling to audiophiles everywhere that it is serious about building the world of Apple Music.

Building a successful brand world

Three essential elements go into building a successful brand world. The first is the Functional Offer, quite simply what the brand makes. For example, Volvo builds cars. The second is the Emotional Promise, which is how a brand is experienced emotionally. Through good Scandinavian design Volvo makes people feel great driving them. The third is the Cultural Quest. This is the way the brand engages with the world or a larger topic it constantly explores. For Volvo it is to have no one die in its vehicles. Ever.

All brands achieve the first; many achieve the second; very few achieve the third. The Music Hall gives Apple a route to achieving all three: not only producing and distributing music (offer), or making people feel good while listening (promise), but in our increasingly isolated, AI-fueled world, bringing music fans together around the highest quality, most authentic audio experiences, be they physical or digital (quest).

The key is cultivating a community engaging in meaningful participation around a brand world that is authentic to music.

The key question is, therefore, “How might Apple cultivate this passionate community of music fans?”

Lean into the community

Apple Music will need to do more than deliver music. It will need to become synonymous with the production and presentation of the world’s finest content. Opening with Elton John was a signal of intent.

Baby Keem, Skye Newman, Troye Sivan, Arlo Parks, Burna Boy, Chase & Status, Charli XCX—a real lineup to be sure. Now imagine that with its war chest, Apple starts attracting more prominent acts, and that music (sounding better recorded on Spatial Audio) is available only through a subscription on Apple Music.

The athletic wear brands like On and Bandit have been very successful with their run clubs. Might Apple establish its version? In partnership with Apple Pay and a major credit card company, early access to events is not hard to imagine. Nor are listening parties at the coolest venues for those who can’t attend events in person.

What’s at stake?

In its services division, Apple has shown with Pay and Health that it likes opportunities with huge addressable markets that plug in nicely to its hardware ecosystem. The global home audio hardware market, whose current players have set a fairly low bar, is valued at approximately $43.2 billion in 2026. If Apple can capture a good share of smart home audio and combine that with a greater share of the $54.54 billion global audio streaming market, the dollars become very real.

By striking the right chord between premium audio hardware and subscription streaming, Apple is perfectly positioned to turn this massive market overlap into music to its investors’ ears.

Palantir Just Made Company History. Is the Stock a Buy?

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Palantir's business is booming, but I'm concerned about the stock.

Palantir (PLTR -2.11%) just did something for the first time during its third-quarter earnings report: Its U.S. commercial revenue surpassed U.S. government revenue.

That's a big deal, because Palantir got its start selling its AI-powered software to the government. As Palantir transitions to this new paradigm, is that a good thing for the company? Or is it a concern?

I think it's a good thing, as the company is spreading its customer base out among different clients that wouldn't be affected by a government spending crackdown. But does that make the stock a buy overall? Let's take a look.

Palantir continues its streak of crushing expectations and outperforming where both management and the analyst community thought results would be. Outperformance is becoming the expectation, as management has consistently underguided and overdelivered every quarter (which isn't a bad thing).

In Q3, Palantir's revenue reached $2.3 billion, up 96% year over year. At the end of Q2, management told investors to expect $2.16 billion in revenue, so this was a sizable beat. Another factor Palantir has going for it is that its net income margin remained strong. It posted a 49% profit margin in Q3, making it one of the more profitable companies on Earth.

If that's the only perspective you have on Palantir's stock, it's easy to conclude that the stock is a strong buy. These results are simply incredible, and doubting Palantir's business isn't a smart move.

However, investing in the stock is a different story.

The price you pay for a stock matters, and even the most impressive company bought at the wrong price tag can lead to underperformance or even negative returns. Palantir's stock (which the company has no control over) is toeing that line.

Palantir's stock trades for a very expensive 110 times this year's earnings estimates and 76 times next year's.

PLTR PE Ratio (Forward 1y) data by YCharts

Somehow, among the broad AI sell-off, Palantir's stock has remained highly valued. Companies on the infrastructure side are trading for less than 20 times next year's earnings, and even the AI hyperscalers are trading in the low 20s. This showcases just how expensive Palantir's stock is, and its day of reckoning could be coming.

I don't think the stock is a buy here, as it's just too expensive compared to some of the other AI investments available. While I'm a huge fan of Palantir, the business, I'm much more concerned about Palantir's stock. As a result, I think it's one to stay avoid.

Keithen Drury has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Palantir Technologies. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

Cristiano Ronaldo international curtain call is a bitter affair and sorry stain on one of the greats' legacies

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

To the very end, Cristiano Ronaldo wants ownership of the narrative.

Ahead of Jorge Jesus' Portugal squad announcement for the upcoming November international break, Ronaldo has officially retired from the international side of the game.

Ronaldo's retirement statement was polished and studio-produced, framing his departure from international football as a considered, dignified decision of a man bowing out on his own terms. But nobody in football should be fooled by the PR surrounding his Portugal farewell. Ronaldo is not walking away because of his obligation to 'hand back the No. 7 shirt'; he is jumping before he's pushed.

With Portugal’s squad announcement for the November international window imminent, whispers had grown impossible to ignore, especially after the histrionics which made headlines during the most recent international break.

For everything we know about Ronaldo and the opinion he holds of himself, the prospect of reading a squad list without his name on it would have been unbearable.

No matter how it's dressed up, his exit from the international scene is an undignified end for a footballer of his stature, burning the house down rather than accept that time had finally caught up with him.

The genesis of the split came during the conjoined September and October international camps, where Ronaldo's conduct reflected entitled petulance. He could have chosen to be an experienced mentor to one of Europe's most gifted squads, but instead behaved emotionally, putting self before side.

The best features, fun and footballing quizzes, straight to your inbox every week.

When substituted early during Portugal's Nations League fixture against Wales, the 41-year-old trudged off, held his tongue but not for very long.

From the outset, Jesus had made it clear that Portugal’s tactical system would no longer be warped to accommodate Ronaldo. The new coach wants aggressive front-foot pressing, off-the-ball running, and quick transitions, which Ronaldo is simply incompatible with. Tensions reportedly grew in training over Ronaldo’s lack of engagement in defensive drills and his habit of dropping deep into midfield spaces, disrupting the collective shape to hunt for touches.

"After the National Team Coach's press conference, and following a conversation with the President of the Portuguese Football Federation, I made the decision to leave the National Team camp," Ronaldo wrote in a statement after being left out entirely against Norway. "In due time, I will tell the truth to all Portuguese people about the reasons that motivated my departure from the national team, to which I have always dedicated myself. Now is the time to wish good luck to Portugal and to all my teammates, without exception."

The ensuing media storm hijacked Portugal’s camp leaving teammates fielding questions about their captain's bruised ego rather than their own football.

That the curtain falls in this manner is a tragedy for international football. His 146 goals, 234 appearances, alarming longevity and bordering-on-delusional drive made him more than a footballer. Even his detractors will concede, he's earned the right to a fairytale farewell, which no doubt would have been a tearful, adoring lap of honour in Lisbon, where his career began over 20 years ago.

Now, though, he slips out through the side exit, some might say motivated by spite and wanting to save face. There will be no emotional testimonial or triumphant send-off, at least not yet. Cristiano Ronaldo may have spared himself the humiliation of being dropped, but in doing so, has traded in his hero’s goodbye.

InfoQ Homepage News Building a Culture for a Kubernetes-Based Platform in a Bank

Nov 05, 2026 3 min read

In a banking environment, open source can earn trust by running real workloads, as Marcy Paramonova and Stéphane Cusin explained in their talk Building Cloud Native Culture in a Bank at KubeCon & CloudNativeCon Europe. Deploying Kubernetes was the easy part; the hard part was culture: community of practice, shared knowledge, rituals, ownership. Teams try things, keep what works, and improve bottom-up.

Open source was not an obvious choice in their banking environment, Cusin mentioned. They received questions about support and accountability. Instead of trying to convince people with theory, they started running real workloads. Over time, trust was built by operating the platform day after day:

Open source stopped being seen as a risk. It became the foundation.

They treated their first version of Kubernetes like a project. "We delivered it, we got a good adoption", Cusin said. But the operation was incredibly hard. Not because the technology was broken, but because they hadn’t built the culture around it yet:

Nobody knew who to call, responsibilities were blurry, and teams were consuming the platform without really contributing to it. Every incident felt like a fire drill.

They realized deploying Kubernetes was the easy part. The hard part was building the culture with a community of practice around it. The shared knowledge, the rituals, the ownership model, Cusin explained:

Without that culture, no platform survives its own success.

They invite people to experiment, for instance by having a yearly competition where everybody can submit their ideas and the one that wins, actually gets implemented. Many of their improvements came from the bottom up, Paramonova argued.

They used open source as a compass, something that helped them orientate a subject, Cusin explained:

When Kubernetes appeared, we recognized it immediately. Strong Kubernetes, transparent governance, and real production users. The compass pointed; we followed. And now we are watching OpenStack the same way.

A compass doesn’t force you in one direction. It helps you recognize the right opportunity when it arrives, Cusin said.

They tried things, and some of them didn’t work. They kept the ones that did. The act of trying, of deliberately designing how teams interact, creates the culture we have today, Cusin said. Structure first, the culture follows.

Every organization is different; every regulated environment has its own constraints, Paramonova said. You need a compass during your journey. It’s something that will guide you when everything else is uncertain. For us, it was open source, she concluded.

InfoQ interviewed Marcy Paramonova and Stéphane Cusin after their talk.

InfoQ: How did you gain trust from your development teams?

Stéphane Cusin: Over time, trust emerged from a combination of transparency and reliability. When developers see that the platform consistently helps them deliver applications safely and efficiently, adoption follows naturally.

Marcy Paramonova: Trust was not something we could establish by presenting a roadmap or writing good documentation, though both matter. It came from running things. From being present when something broke. From being honest about what was not yet ready.

Cusin: Today, our culture is centered around ownership, automation, transparency, and continuous improvement. Teams are empowered to move quickly, but they do so within a framework of shared standards and platform capabilities. The result is not only greater efficiency, but also greater consistency, reliability, and visibility across the organization.

InfoQ: How did Kubernetes transform how your organization builds software?

Cusin: Kubernetes gave us a common platform and a common operating model. In the first version of our platform, we gave teams flexibility. We exposed many Kubernetes capabilities directly and allowed teams to customize their deployment model extensively. In practice, that often resulted in different patterns emerging across teams.

One of the lessons we learned as platform engineers is that developers do not come to a platform looking for more infrastructure choices. They come to a platform to deliver software.

In our current platform, Kubernetes is no longer the product. Kubernetes is the implementation detail. The product is a simple, secure, and standardized software delivery experience.

We provide opinionated deployment patterns, standardized Helm charts, and common platform capabilities. As a result, when an engineer moves from one team to another, the deployment experience is largely the same. They do not need to relearn how applications are packaged, deployed, monitored, or operated.

Paramonova: The transformation I experienced most directly was organizational. Kubernetes gave teams a common language. It created shared abstractions that made collaboration across different parts of engineering much more natural.

Before, a lot of knowledge was siloed by team or by technology stack. Kubernetes did not erase those silos overnight, but it gave everyone a shared foundation to build on. That changed conversations, and changed what was possible.

Democrats reportedly seek greater SEC control over crypto after CLARITY Act collapse

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The proposed rewrite could expand SEC oversight and revive stricter rules on crypto withdrawals, custody, proof of reserves, and investor protection.

House Democrats are reportedly discussing a broader crypto market structure bill built partly on Rep. Maxine Waters' earlier investor-rights proposals.

According to a Nov. 4 post by journalist Eleanor Terrett, the proposals include a larger SEC role, a smaller CFTC role, and more government ethics restrictions.

Industry sources who spoke with Democratic staff form the basis of that account, and the bill text remains unpublished. Waters' 2025 amendment shows what customer rules a Democratic framework could carry, and the September CLARITY compromise shows how much of that ground the Senate text already covered before cloture failed 49-50 on Sept. 15.

Amendment #31, a nine-page text from July 2025, would have imposed duties on exchanges, brokers, custodians, and other retail-facing digital asset firms. Withdrawals and transfers would be due within 24 hours, except in cases of documented good-faith fraud.

Firms would join SIPC if eligible or fund an equivalent customer-asset protection arrangement, hold customer assets in segregated, bankruptcy-remote accounts, and obtain affirmative, revocable consent before rehypothecating, lending, or staking them.

Cryptographically verifiable proof of reserves would include a licensed accountant's monthly attestation.

Crypto customer orders would take priority over proprietary orders under best-execution practices, and affiliated proprietary traders or market makers would be barred from trading against customers on the same platform.

Human support would be reachable around the clock within five minutes, and customers and regulators would hear of specified security breaches within 48 hours.

The text added a one-page fee disclosure, next-business-day settlement, and an indicator showing the share of customers holding an asset at a loss. Its insurance language covered losses from a covered entity's regulatory violations through bonds, insurance or insured trusts, a narrower promise than compensating every market loss.

The House Rules Committee voted 8-4 on July 14, 2025 against making the amendment eligible for floor consideration, and the amendment died in committee.

The Congressional Research Service's Sept. 15 analysis describes customer-property protections, limits on commingling and bankruptcy treatment for digital asset customers.

It also lists CFTC registration for exchanges, brokers and dealers, core principles covering crypto market manipulation, conflicts and customer protection, and Bank Secrecy Act duties.

The SEC kept a role in investment-contract offerings, including an exemption for qualifying token originators up to $200 million with accompanying disclosures.

Sens. Cynthia Lummis, John Boozman and Tim Scott said their Sept. 14 revision included 126 substantive revisions requested by Democrats.

Among them were ethics provisions drawing on the Tillis-Gallego approach with state attorney general enforcement, and Treasury authority to restrict stablecoin incentives when bank deposit outflows are large.

The figure is the sponsors' tally, which stands apart from Democratic endorsement of the text. The ethics text required certain covered officials with large financial interests to divest or use blind trusts, and the sides remained divided over its scope and enforceability.

Start with the stories that matter. Get the context behind the headlines and a clear view of what to watch next.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

Waters' text set a uniform 24-hour deadline and a 12-per-year attestation schedule, affirmative consent for staking and lending, customer-first order priority and affiliated-trading bans, and compensation mechanisms.

September described principles, and a Democratic successor could turn principles into enforceable rights with deadlines, reporting standards, and penalties.

The CFTC oversees derivatives and has antifraud and antimanipulation authority over spot commodity markets, but full spot exchange supervision needs new statute, which September's draft sought to provide.

A Democratic text could add SEC oversight of intermediaries, issuers or customer standards, or redraw the definitions that sort activity between agencies.

Exchanges would face added registration, disclosure, order-handling, and custody duties; custodians would face higher segregation and verification standards; and smaller platforms would face higher fixed compliance costs.

The March 2026 SEC and CFTC interpretation treats Bitcoin as a digital commodity, and exchange regulation and token classification are separate legal matters.

A customer holding BTC on an exchange would be affected by withdrawal deadlines, reserve verification, and insolvency treatment, while a self-custody holder has a different exposure.

PCAOB investor-advocate staff has cautioned that ordinary proof-of-reserve reports differ from full audits and can omit liabilities, ownership rights, and borrowed assets.

SIPC says non-security crypto assets sit outside its protection even at a SIPC-member brokerage, so an equivalent mechanism would need its own terms on eligible entities, limits, and funding.

Take a hypothetical exchange holding $1 billion of customer crypto that can execute transfers equal to 15% of balances in a day. A 20% withdrawal rush leaves a $50 million processing gap, and a 40% rush leaves $250 million.

The gap reflects operations at a fully backed firm, and the inputs are assumptions. The Waters text also set one-year effectiveness, up to 18 months for joint SEC and CFTC rules and a possible extra year for small entities.

Cloture on the motion to proceed needed 60 votes and drew 49 in favor and 50 against, with four Republicans voting no. The vote concerned proceeding to the bill, and a passage vote awaits any revival.

Any successor needs identical text from both chambers and a presidential signature or a veto override. Open items include the split of SEC and CFTC authority, the cost of new exchange obligations, DeFi and developer treatment, and bank worries over stablecoin incentives and ethics enforcement.

Agencies can act within existing law, as the SEC did with its August Regulation Crypto Assets proposal, the Sept. 17 Innovation Exemption and the Oct. 1 custody proposal, whose comments are due Dec. 7. Compensation schemes and jurisdiction over all spot markets need statute.

If lawmakers pair CFTC spot oversight with explicit customer standards, initial compliance costs rise, and regulatory certainty lasts longer than agency guidance.

If jurisdiction and ethics deadlock continues, customers depend on existing law, state rules, and each platform's terms, and a major custody failure could force faster, harsher rules.

The next crypto fight may hinge more on whether ordinary investors gain enforceable rights when an exchange delays a withdrawal or fails.

This Startup Wants To Turn Teslas Into Anything, From 11-Seat Minibuses To Pickups

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Browse this and many other websites without banner ads, personalized tracking and video ads.

The slightly dorky vehicle you see here can use Tesla’s Superchargers and so-called Full-Self Driving (Supervised) driver assistance system. And no, your eyes are not deceiving you—this is not a Tesla. At least, not at first glance.

It’s called the Hoffman Badger, and it’s the creation of a small Wisconsin startup that wants to turn old Tesla EVs into pretty much any type of vehicle. The first Badger made its debut at this year’s SEMA show in Las Vegas, driving on its own wheels all the way from its home base and using Superchargers along the way, just like any Tesla would.

Hoffman says it uses all of the hardware and software from the donor vehicle, which can be either the Model 3 or Model Y. The seats, dashboard, steering wheel, motors, and even the door handles are lifted from Tesla. Then, they’re installed in a new chassis that puts the high-voltage battery pack one and a half inches higher than in the original vehicles. This, along with the eight-inch ground clearance, improves the boxy EV’s breakover angle, making it better suited for rough roads.

The startup says it can build around 100 units per year, but exact details haven’t been ironed out yet. In an Out of Spec Review video, Geoffrey Hoffman, the company’s founder, said that he doesn’t have a precise plan for the short term, but that attending SEMA will help in identifying what people actually want.

Hoffman expects the pricing to be somewhere between the Slate Truck and the Tesla Cybertruck, so from $26,400 all the way up to $77,000, if the base dual-motor Cybertruck is the upper target.

The doors, the roof, and most of the seats are removable, and the vehicle is titled as an 11-seat bus. All of the structure and body parts are powder-coated aluminum, while the exposed fasteners are stainless steel or steel coated with 1,000-hour salt spray resistance. Inside, the fasteners are stainless or zinc-plated steel. All of the new materials are recycled, except for a few small 3D-printed parts.

If and when sales eventually kick off, the Badger will be available as a ready-made vehicle with whatever top hat the customer wants, or as a kit with a VIN. In the latter case, the customer will have to source a donor Tesla Model 3 or Model Y.

There’s no estimated range, but seeing how it’s shaped like a brick, it will most likely be less than the donor car’s original figure. That said, Hoffman said that its boxy EV may deliver more range around town compared to the Model 3 or Model Y, simply because the Badger is lighter. In the first quarter of 2027, the aerodynamics will be improved, according to the startup.

Hoffman’s main business is converting old British cars to electric. Its portfolio includes the DeLorean DMC-12, the classic Volkswagen Beetle, classic Land Rovers, and vintage Rolls-Royce and Bentley models.

2 Bank Stocks to Buy After Their Third-Quarter Earnings Reports

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

With the third-quarter earnings season well underway, most banks have now reported.

Largely speaking, bank profits have continued to climb, as most banks take advantage of higher interest rates and a steep yield curve, in which lower-maturity bonds yield less than higher-maturity ones, an environment that most banks tend to benefit from.

Banks typically borrow money at short-term rates and lend it out at longer-term rates, which is why banks benefit from a steeper yield curve. Still, it's not all roses. Banks are also grappling with higher funding costs, while investors wonder whether a real credit cycle will ever show up.

Here are two bank stocks to buy after their third-quarter earnings reports.

For investors just starting to look at bank stocks, or who simply want a little exposure, JPMorgan Chase (JPM +4.18%) is an easy place to begin. It's the country's largest bank, with more than $5 trillion in assets, and is run by Chief Executive Officer Jamie Dimon, whom many regard as the best leader in banking.

JPMorgan Chase is good at many different banking businesses, from credit card lending to investment banking to wealth management and payments.

So you've got a bank that can benefit from a steep yield curve like other traditional banks, but JPMorgan will also benefit from large initial public offerings like the one planned for artificial intelligence (AI) company Anthropic, which only a handful of the country's largest investment banks can say. This gives JPMorgan revenue diversity that few banks have.

JPMorgan actually missed Wall Street estimates for both earnings and revenue in the third quarter, which is rare for the bank. The undershot came from weaker fixed-income trading revenue and higher expenses.

However, JPMorgan raised its full-year outlook for net interest income (NII), the spread revenue the bank makes on its loans and bond holdings after covering its funding costs for the deposits and other interest-bearing liabilities that fund those assets.

Management provided a forecast of $107 billion for NII in 2027, representing roughly a 10% increase over its projected 2026 NII. While JPMorgan trades at a premium valuation of roughly 3 times its tangible book value (TBV), or its theoretical liquidation value, it has earned this premium during two decades of excellence.

Investors should ignore the third-quarter earnings miss and buy the stock for the long haul.

Raleigh, North Carolina-based First Citizens BancShares (FCNCA +0.41%) has total assets of roughly $241 billion, putting it at a fraction of the size of JPMorgan Chase, but still one of the larger banks in the country.

First Citizens has delivered a masterclass in growth through acquisition, particularly by purchasing distressed banks or those on the brink of failure.

For instance, First Citizens acquired the struggling CIT Group in 2022, which traded at a significant discount to its TBV. As a result, the deal was 38% accretive to First Citizens' TBV, which is a huge increase. Most bank acquisitions dilute TBV and take two or three years to earn back the dilution.

First Citizens struck again when it acquired all of Silicon Valley Bank's deposits, loans, and branches after the Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation (FDIC) took over during the banking crisis in 2023, of which Silicon Valley Bank was at the center.

Shares of First Citizens soared about 40% after announcing the acquisition. Furthermore, First Citizens had a loss-sharing agreement with the FDIC on loans, as well as other protections that made the deal even more appealing.

After its third-quarter earnings on Oct. 23, First Citizens' stock rose nearly 8% after adjusted earnings per share of $55.37 easily topped estimates of $45.89, according to FactSet. The bank's board also approved a $1.2 billion share repurchase plan through next year.

First Citizens is a proven acquirer, and I suspect it will continue to grow in this manner.

"Ryan’s going to do his thing with it, and I’m looking forward to seeing it," said Cage to The Hollywood Reporter. The star revealed that he has already reached out to Gosling to pass on his congratulations, adding, "Ryan knows how I feel about him as an actor; we’ve had nice conversations, and we stay in touch. I was happy for him and wished him the best of luck on his adventures, and I look forward to seeing the movie.”

Cage starred as the fire-headed motorcycle stuntman in the 2007 movie Ghost Rider, following stunt motorcyclist Johnny Blaze who makes a deal with the Devil to save his father, but years later he is granted special powers so he can destroy the Devil's son, Blackheart. Funnily enough, the original movie also stars Eva Mendes as Blaze's love interest, who is Gosling's wife in real life. Cage reprised the role in the 2012 sequel Ghost Rider: Spirit of Vengeance, but Blaze has been off our screens ever since.

Blaze was first introduced to Marvel Comics in 1972. After gaining his powers, the flaming skull head rides around on his fiery bike, going after criminals and punishing them in violent ways with his demonic powers. No plot details for the MCU Ghost Rider movie have been announced at this time, so it is not clear if it will act as an origin story as Cage's 2007 flick did. The new movie is set to start production in New York next year

Marvel announced the upcoming Ghost Rider movie earlier this year at San Diego Comic-Con 2026. Deadpool and Wolverine helmer Shawn Levy is set to direct, with Jonathan Tropper on board as writer. Heated Rivalry star Connor Storrie is in talks to play the movie's villain, and Marvel fans think he might be playing Johnny Blaze's long-lost brother and fellow Ghost Rider, Danny Ketch.

Ghost Rider is set to hit theaters on July 28, 2028. For more, keep up with other upcoming Marvel movies and shows.

Bluesky was right all along and will accept your apology any time

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Bluesky is a niche Twitter-clone frequently accused of being an echo chamber for progressives. After the blue tsunami of this week’s midterms, however, its inhabitants don’t seem to be echoing themselves so much as the vast majority of American voters.

The sheer scale of Democrats’ victory is staggering. They won control of both chambers of the U.S. Congress, flipping Senate seats in seven states Trump carried in 2024—including Republican stronghold Texas, which now also has a Democratic governor and attorney general. All in all, this year’s midterms represent the biggest electoral shift in state-level politics to hit the U.S. in generations. They also represent a Texas-sized validation of the worldview Bluesky has become notorious for over the past two years.

Although it officially launched in the spring of 2023, Bluesky might as well have been born on election eve in 2024. That was the moment millions of disillusioned Twitter users decamped from a website owned by the largest single contributor to Trump’s reelection. Bluesky quickly became a haven for Twitter expats and grew at a frenetic pace. It went from 9 million users in September 2024 to 15 million by mid-November, eventually reaching its current 47 million.

Because many of Bluesky’s users arrived out of principled opposition to X, formerly Twitter, the two sites have arguably existed in opposition to each other. Both portray their counterpart as out of touch, with Bluesky alleging Elon Musk’s algorithm tweaks have skewed X’s political vortex too far to the right, and X suggesting Bluesky is a bubble of liberals who can tolerate anything except a difference in opinion.

The latter idea seemed to largely prevail. 

In 2025, Washington Post columnists, tech world figures like Mark Cuban, and myriad other pundits dismissed Bluesky as smug, self-righteous, and irrelevant. It was the fantasy sandbox where progressives went to play among themselves, protecting reality dwellers from having to hear their drivel. (“Like a containment dome over a nuclear reactor,” as the writer Josh Barro put it.)

So, how well did Bluesky’s users stay in touch with reality between elections, compared with X’s exponentially larger population? Consider the post-midterms perspective.

Rather than accept the narrative that 2024 represented a rejection of “wokeness” and a political realignment toward Trump, the manifestly trans-friendly Bluesky suggested the election was more a referendum on postpandemic inflation and global anti-incumbent bias. As an emboldened Trump then proceeded to antagonize U.S. citizens and the rest of the world, while making billions for himself in crypto, Bluesky meticulously chronicled the state of play. It became the premier destination for seeing radicalized older white ladies, abysmally attended pro-ICE rallies, and complaints about the lack of mainstream news coverage around massive No Kings protests. Any random day would find endless posts with evidence that, per the motto of the political philosopher and Bluesky power user Olúfẹ́mi O. Táíwò, “We are going to win.” 

Above all, Bluesky proved to be less a bubble than an expression of the multitude of ideological factions that had united under the umbrella of non-MAGAness . . . to argue with one another.

Meanwhile, X’s millions of liberal and progressive folks were often drowned out by an algorithm that seemed to favor right-wing voices and their preferred narratives. A bubble chart from Nate Silver, the political analyst, visually depicted the accounts with highest engagement rates back in April. Its many MAGA-red circles made it look like the blood-filled elevator from The Shining. As a May 2026 study from Northwestern University suggests, X’s algorithm rewards posts that provoke strong reactions, so the loudest and most emotionally satisfying political claims become disproportionately visible. That’s how we got viral fever dreams about James Talarico, the then-senatorial candidate, checking into a mental hospital, something that never in fact happened.

It should come as little surprise that many high-profile MAGA voices on X spent the days leading up to the election incorrectly predicting how it would turn out.

Who looks out of touch and stuck in an echo chamber now?

While X may have been more plugged into the political discourse online for the past two years, Bluesky now appears better attuned to what’s been happening on the ground. Its users were right about pretty much everything. 

They were right that anti-trans ads would have little or no impact on the 2026 election, and that their impact on the previous election had been vastly overstated.

They were right that Graham Platner wasn’t the only Democrat who could beat Susan Collins in Maine, and that Abdul El-Sayed’s association with a controversial Twitch streamer relatively few voters had even heard of was not as big a deal as X users seemed to think. 

And they were right that fleeing X’s algorithm helped them view the world with clear eyes.

If Bluesky is a bubble, then it is currently a champagne bubble awash in celebration.

The festivities will likely last only a matter of hours, though, until infighting explodes around just how the Democrats should spend their newfound political capital.

6 good, bad, and weird changes in The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time for Switch 2

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

You may have heard by now that Nintendo has remade mighty '90s classic The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time. The reimagining of this 1998 Nintendo 64 adventure game for Switch 2 includes many major and minor changes, not all of them created equal.

With the Ocarina remake on shelves now, I wanted to highlight a handful of differences that stood out to me while playing. Some of them are fun, others are frustrating, and some defy that sort of categorization entirely. Without wasting any more time, let's dive in.

I'm not going to be too specific about this change because the game literally just came out and I don't want to ruin the experience for folks.

That said, you should know going in that Nintendo chose to alter several of Ocarina's legendary boss fights with new attacks, animations, and more. It's one of the most exciting elements of the new game for me, as it adds a sense of novelty to the adventure that is otherwise lacking, owing to the game's status as a remake. Every time a well-known boss fight swerved and surprised me, I had a huge grin on my face.

One of the best puzzle-solving items in the entire Zelda series is the hookshot, a straight-shooting grapple gun that can easily zip Link between points. Ocarina was not the first game to incorporate the hookshot, but it did expand the tool's usefulness with an extended targeting range and a wide variety of surfaces to which the hookshot can attach, such as wooden treasure chests and the rooftops of Kakariko Village.

Unfortunately, that philosophy has not been entirely maintained in the Switch 2 remake. Most grapple points remain intact, but many have been changed from natural-feeling features like tree branches to clearly marked targets. I don't mind that change so much as I mind the fact that the hookshot can no longer grapple onto Kakariko's rooftops. This greatly reduces Link's ability to move around the village and just makes the game less fun for no discernible reason.

In 2005, Resident Evil 4 set a template for third-person, over-the-shoulder aiming cameras that pretty much every mainstream video game nowadays deploys, including the Ocarina remake. When aiming items like the hookshot, bow, or boomerang in the new Switch 2 release, the camera sits behind Link, slightly offset so his character model doesn't get in the way. You can also aim using gyro controls as a nice bonus.

This point of view works just fine and may be more familiar to younger Zelda fans used to the aiming functionality in 2017's The Legend of Zelda: Breath of the Wild, however, I do miss being able to zoom in with a first-person lens to examine Link's surroundings up close. It's kind of a bummer in the opening Kokiri Forest area, where you can no longer get a good look at the stick drawing on the trunk of Link's treehouse.

I wouldn't say that rupees (the currency in every Zelda game) are totally superfluous in the original Ocarina of Time. After all, you need 40 of them to bypass an early-game obstacle, and some important mini-games require small sums in order to participate. You can also use them to stock up on supplies like arrows and health potions, but frankly, the original version doled out those things generously enough to render that unnecessary.

While I would say Ocarina on Switch 2 is easier overall, the developers did add some new incentives to hoard rupees and collect Gold Skulltula tokens in order to expand the size of Link's wallet. Specifically, the Great Fairies who provide Link with game-changing upgrades now ask for sizable monetary donations before they'll hand over the good stuff. In the original game, the upgrades were free, but some of them cost hundreds of rupees in the remake.

I like this change because it gives players a reason to more fully engage with optional content and wring as much fun out of the reimagined game as possible.

I talked about this in my review, so I'll keep it brief here, but major story cutscenes in the Ocarina remake have been expanded with full voice acting for every character except for Link. The acting itself is a little cartoonish, which adequately fits the vibe, but also comes across as slightly cheesy at times.

More importantly, though, this makes some cutscenes last longer than they might have if they were broken up by text boxes, as they were in the original. Maybe someone can do a proper stopwatch comparison, but it at least felt like the Ocarina remake seized cutscene control for lengthier periods of time than the N64 version did.

Lastly, Nintendo has opted to lengthen the day/night cycle in the Ocarina remake. It was very short in the N64 original, as you could hardly cross Hyrule Field on foot without the sun going down and horrible skeleton monsters emerging to ruin your travels.

In the Switch 2 remake, it takes much longer for the sun to rise and set, which has benefits and drawbacks. On the plus side, you get more time to enjoy each distinct atmosphere, which gives music lead and sound director Masato Ohashi an excuse to craft new, chilled out nighttime versions of some of Zelda composer Koji Kondo's iconic area themes. Some downsides, however, are that the Hyrule Field drawbridge no longer goes up at night, and it's somewhat more tedious to wait for certain times of day in the few situations where that's necessary.

One last note is that the day/night cycle is now universal, rather than turning off in towns and cities like it did in the original game. That means you get to see NPCs actively move from their daytime positions to their nighttime states in real-time, which is pretty neat.

The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time’s Changes to the Infamous Water Temple Are Actually Brilliant

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Zelda's most infamous dungeon is no longer a frustrating rite of passage

With The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time’s Switch 2 remake out now, long-time Nintendo fans are in for a lot of warm nostalgia. A trek through the lost woods, duets with Sheik, a return to the Bombchu Bowling Alley — all of those wonderful moments return in a faithful remake that feels every bit as magical as the Nintendo 64 classic. But with all those wonderful moments comes a painful reunion with one of the most hated moments in Zelda history. Yes, you will have to return to the dreaded Water Temple.

Don’t worry! Even though the Ocarina of Time remake is careful not to change too much about the original game, it does make some small tweaks to a dungeon that still haunts a generation. It’s mercifully easier to navigate now thanks to a few minor but smart updates that go a long way.

The Water Temple is the most infamous dungeon in the original Ocarina of Time, so much so that it has been a bit of a running joke for decades now. The gist is that it’s a four-story structure built around a central room, and it’s flooded with water. To access certain areas, you need to find rooms in the dungeon that allow you to change the water level throughout the temple. Easy enough on paper, but it’s a bit of a pain in practice. Due to the limited visual information you get in-game, it requires you to keep a mental map of where each water adjustment point is, as well as how each of the three levels will change the layout of the dungeon. It’s a headache to keep everything straight, thus earning it a reputation as a frustrating rite of passage.

While that structure hasn’t changed much in the remake, Nintendo does make it easier to get through with a few smart decisions. That came at the request of Eiji Aonuma, who directed the original Ocarina of Time. “I had some overdue homework: I wanted to do something about the Water Temple I created 28 years ago,” Aonuma said in a recent Nintendo developer interview. “Since it became well known for its difficulty in the original game, I had the team make some adjustments in this version.”

One of those adjustments just comes down to clearer signposting. Building off the dungeon’s Ocarina of Time 3D redesign, the doors that lead to water controls are now clearly marked with symbols showing you exactly how the water level will change. You no longer have to remember which door leads to the room that drains the room, or which one will get you to floor two.

On top of that, each of the routes are now color-coded. You’ll find variously colored pipes around each of the three doors, and those visually lead you to the control room if you follow them through the dungeon. For instance, when you enter the route to the room that brings the water to its lowest level, you’ll notice that some blue pipes signal that you should float to the water’s surface. Follow that hint and you’ll find that the pipes lead to clearly marked pedestals for you to play your ocarina on. All of that makes it significantly easier to remember exactly where you need to go at all times.

Arguably, the more impactful Water Temple change comes from a top-level control decision that just so happens to make life easier in the dungeon. Part of why the Water Temple is such a pain is because you need to use a few key items throughout it. Your ocarina, Iron Boots, Hookshot, and more all play major roles in solving puzzles throughout the temple. Since the original game only gave you three item slots to work with, you were often left juggling around your item shortcuts in the main menu. It’s a minor pain point, but one that stands out when you’re constantly swapping items.

While the Switch 2 remake still has its own item management quirks, it wisely keeps your ocarina mapped to the D-pad at all times. Your boots get a quick swap button on the down button there too, meaning that you don’t need to waste an item slot on the Iron Boots. With those two necessities free up, you can dedicate your ZR item slot to the Hookshot and only swap to other items as needed with a quick menu on the R button. That decision cuts out any need for pause-menu management, allowing you to move through the dungeon more fluidly.

For the ‘90s purists, these changes might sound a little sacrilegious. As much as players claim to hate the Water Temple, that complaint invariably comes with a fondness for a genuinely memorable bit of frustrating design. “Why shouldn’t today’s youths struggle through the Water Temple like their elders once did?” you may ask. I, for one, welcome the help, just as I support the idea of today’s students having their loans canceled even though I had to pay mine off. The new visual design tweak makes for some sensible signposting that still feels part of the dungeon’s architecture, and the control solution just makes it easier to stay immersed in a well-built dungeon. New generations of players needn’t suffer like us old Zelda fans once did. Swim free, ye tadpoles.

Nintendo’s excellent Switch 2 remake recaptures the soul of a masterpiece

Three more years for Doncaster Rovers' 'heartbeat' as another key player commits to new deal

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Midfielder Robbie Gotts has agreed a new contract committing him to until the summer of 2029.

Manager Grant McCann sees it as a coup for the League One club, who tied captain Owen Bailey down for another three years in the summer.

Did you know with a Digital subscription to Yorkshire Post, you can get access to all of our premium content, as well as benefiting from fewer ads, loyalty rewards and much more.

Gotts came through at Leeds United, but since moving to what is now called the Club Doncaster Sports Village from Barrow in 2025, he has carved out a role as a mainstay of the side alongside fellow midfielder Bailey.

"He epitomises everything we're about as a football club," said McCann. "He's hard working, energetic and someone who is always looking to improve.

“I'd like to thank Terry (Bramall, the club's owner) and Gavin (Baldwin, chief executive) for their support in making this happen.

"Robbie still had time remaining on his previous contract, but we're delighted to have secured his future for the longer term. It allows him to settle, enjoy his football and continue being an important part of what we're building here.

“He's been the heartbeat of the team over the last six or seven months and has continuously improved. The supporters love watching Gottsy play, and that's because he drives the mentality we want here. It's not just what he brings on the pitch, but the way he trains every day as well."

The 26-year-old, who joined Doncaster after four successful years at Barrow, took a little while to settle in the team last season but 44 appearances in all competitions, and Saturday's defeat at Milton Keynes Dons – when he came on as a half-time substitute – was the first league game he has not started this season.

He has impressed McCann off the field too.

“Robbie is like a sponge," he said. "You give him information and he absorbs it, takes it on board and goes out and delivers. He wants to learn, he wants to improve and he's always looking at ways to develop his game.

"He's got so many attributes we really like, and while there are still little things we want to add to his game, his energy, hard work, aggression and ability to look after the ball make him a fantastic player to work with.

“It's important that we reward people for their performances, and this is a real sign to the rest of the squad that if you come in and perform to a high standard, you'll be rewarded. We've worked hard to get ahead of the game with our contract planning, and this is another example of that.”

The desire to keep improving was high in Gotts’ mind when he talked about his new deal.

“Breaking into the team last season was a big moment for me and since then I've just wanted to keep improving and enjoying every match,” he said. “I've worked hard on my game, both on and off the pitch, and I feel like I've developed a lot since joining.

“I want to keep driving my own standards, getting better every day and helping the team as much as I can. We've got a great group of players and people at the club, and I genuinely believe we've got the togetherness and quality to push up the table.

“The supporters have been brilliant with me since I've been here, and it's always special to hear them singing my name. I want to keep giving everything for them and hopefully we can achieve some really good things together.”

Scarlett Johansson has broken her silence on The Batman 2, with the one-time Marvel star revealing she found it hard to crack her mystery DC role.

“It gave me a lot of existential angst, for some reason,” Johansson said in an interview with Entertainment Tonight.

On finding her way into the character, she added, "It was very hard to figure out. I don’t know why, whether it was just the role or it was just where I am in my life, but it was just hard to crack it. That’s all I can say about it. It was a tough role in a lot of ways."

Right now, most of The Batman sequel is under lock and key. We know that director Matt Reeves had to depart the production, with Steven Soderbergh stepping in to complete Reeves's work.

Playstation and Spider-Man performance see Sony profits surge

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Sony reported Q2 net profits of $2.84 billion, a 44 percent jump from the same period last year.﻿

Sony reported Q2 net profits of $2.84 billion, a 44 percent jump from the same period last year.﻿

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Sony is enjoying a sharp profit spike in its latest quarterly financials, largely driven by its PlayStation business and the blockbuster success of Spider-Man: Brand New Day. According to the company’s September earnings, Sony’s net profits climbed to 449.60 billion yen (about $2.84 billion) for the three months ended September — a 44 percent increase compared to the 311.4 billion yen ($1.96 billion) it reported for the same period last year.

Spider-Man: Brand New Day was a notable contributor, which released in July and became the third-highest grossing movie in history by earning $2.52 billion at the global box office. That’s a huge boost for Sony Pictures Entertainment, which posted earnings of $581 million for the three months ended September — a 527 percent jump from the $93 million it reported for the same period in 2025.

Sales of Playstation 5 consoles have fallen, with 2.6 million units reported this second quarter compared to the 4 million that were sold in Q2 2025. While the Sony-exclusive Marvel’s Wolverine sold 2.4 million units against a reported budget of $300 million, PlayStation game sales generally have increased for both digital downloads and physical discs. That’s in spite of the week-long player blackout that took place in August to protest Sony’s plan to ditch discs entirely. Looking forward, PlayStation is now looking at its next generation console, which developers are making “solid progress” on according to Sony CFO Lin Tao, and is “progressing as scheduled.”

A half-built neutrino telescope on the floor of the Mediterranean Sea searched for cracks in one of physics' most basic invariances. It came up empty, but its limits rival those of detectors many times its size.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The view from inside a detector building block, with the yellow buoy of a nearby detection unit clearly visible.

A particle-detecting telescope sunk more than a mile beneath the sea off the coast of France has tested a core principle of Einstein's relativity and found no cracks.

The detector, called Oscillation Research with Cosmics in the Abyss (ORCA), detects elusive particles called neutrinos. As a doctoral student, Alba Domi rode onto the Mediterranean aboard one of two boats that helped build the telescope, lowering some of its first detection units to the seafloor about 1.5 miles (2.4 kilometers) below the waves off Toulon, France.

Now, data from those first detection units have been used to test one of the founding rules of Einstein’s relativity: the idea that the laws of nature look the same no matter which way you face or how fast you move.

The team found no sign that this rule, called Lorentz invariance, is broken. But even with a detector only a fraction of its planned size, they set limits that compete with those of far larger and longer-running experiments.

The results were published Wednesday (Nov. 4) in the Journal of Cosmology and Astroparticle Physics.

Physics rests on two successful theories: general relativity, conceived by Albert Einstein more than 100 years ago, and the Standard Model of subatomic physics.

"General relativity describes the universe well on a cosmic scale, and the Standard Model of particle physics describes the microscopic scale; really, the single particles, how they behave," said study co-author Lukas Hennig, a doctoral student in advanced physics at Erlangen-Nürnberg.

Get the world’s most fascinating discoveries delivered straight to your inbox.

Both theories assume Lorentz invariance to be true. But in extreme settings, such as the first moments after the Big Bang, the two theories don't mesh. That has fueled a decades-long hunt for a theory of quantum gravity that unites them. Some candidates, including versions of string theory and loop quantum gravity, suggest that space-time might have a hidden "grain" at the tiniest scales — and that Lorentz invariance, "one of the most basic assumptions we make about our universe, might be violated," Hennig said.

Any such violation would be extremely small. To spot it, physicists would need a probe that could travel long distances and barely interact with anything along the way. Enter the neutrino.

Neutrinos are nearly massless particles that pass through Earth every second and carry zero charge. They barely interact with anything and come in three types: electron, muon and tau neutrinos. As neutrinos travel, they morph from one type into another in a predictable pattern that physicists call neutrino oscillation.

An optical module attached to the Dyneema ropes of the detection unit.

If Lorentz invariance were broken, that oscillation would change, with the effect growing more pronounced the farther the neutrinos traveled. So the team focused on neutrinos created when cosmic rays slam into the atmosphere elsewhere on the globe before traveling up through the planet to the detector. Some of the tested neutrinos crossed the entire 7,900-mile (12,700 km) width of Earth.

ORCA is part of the KM3NeT neutrino telescope. Each of its detection units is anchored to the seabed and holds 18 glass spheres packed with 31 light sensors apiece. When a neutrino collides with a particle in the water, it produces a faint flash of blue light that the sensors catch. The many sensors in each sphere help the team pin down which direction the light came from, Domi said.

Earth itself serves as a filter. The researchers selected only particles traveling upward through the planet, and they were able to weed out the flood of other particles from the atmosphere that would otherwise swamp the signal. "Only the neutrinos can get through," Hennig said.

That setup differs from that of IceCube, the Nobel Prize-winning detector buried in Antarctic ice. "Ice scatters the light more than water, but on the other hand, water absorbs the light more than ice," Domi said, which changes how each team reconstructs a neutrino's energy and path.

The researchers used 5,828 neutrinos collected over about 17 months between 2020 and 2021. They found that the oscillations matched standard physics. That means if Lorentz violation exists, it must be weaker than the limits the team set.

Those limits matter for theorists. If a model "predicts a Lorentz invariance violation [stronger than the new limits], the data rule it out. In that case, you can be pretty sure that this model is not correct," Hennig said.

The team also set the first direct limits on a kind of violation that would treat muon neutrinos and tau neutrinos differently.

The detector's limits on several key measures are competitive with those from Japan's Super-Kamiokande, which used 12 years of data, and from IceCube, which used two years of data from its full array.

"I'm surprised that we have competitive results with only six detection units," Hennig said. Today, 44 units are in the water, and the collaboration plans to activate 108 by 2030.

The best tests may still lie ahead. Because Lorentz-violating effects grow with distance and energy, neutrinos from distant cosmic sources are "actually the best candidates to do this kind of research," Domi said. The team plans to repeat the search using ORCA's sister detector, ARCA (Astroparticle Research with Cosmics in the Abyss), which sits off the coast of Sicily and is designed to catch those high-energy cosmic travelers.

If space-time does have a grain, those particles may be among the first to feel it.

Adriani et al. (2026). Atmospheric neutrino constraints on Lorentz invariance violation with the first six detection units of KM3NET/ORCA. Journal of Cosmology and Astroparticle Physics. https://doi.org/10.1088/1475-7516/2026/11/012

Anthropic loosens Claude's game cloning ban — AI will now offer to build original games in the requested genre

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

The developers must balance Claude's capabilities with the need to avoid 'pirating' games.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The explosion of cloning and reverse-engineering games with AI has forced Anthropic to completely block its AI tools from executing these tasks. However, this has also negatively impacted game developers, who have started complaining that they can no longer use the tool for their own legitimate purposes. The Anthropic team has updated the rule so that Claude will now offer to build an original game in the same genre when asked to copy an existing one, according to an X post by technical staff member Addy Osmani.

"Last week some of you ran into Claude Code pushing back on game projects it should have just built. We'd added a short rule to the Opus 5.5 prompt to steer Claude away from recreating specific existing games, but it was also kicking in when you referenced one just to describe the mechanics you wanted," Osmani wrote. "We've updated that rule in 2.1.317. You can name a game to describe what you want, and Claude should now build an original game in that genre. If you ask for a copy of the original, it'll offer to build something similar instead."

Game decompiling used to be difficult task — often taking teams of dedicated and skilled volunteers months, if not years, to complete. The availability of AI tools has made this process so much faster and easier, although that doesn’t automatically mean that the decompiled version comes out clean. But what’s worse is that some bad actors have been using the technology to reverse engineer game demos from small and indie developers and blackmailing them by threatening to release an AI-completed version of their game — holding the developers’ games hostage and asking for ransom so that their work is not spoiled or destroyed.

Anthropic engineers are now trying to find a balance between preventing its tools from being used to copy games and earning the ire of software giants, publishers, and game developers while also ensuring that its paying customers can continue to use Claude for their own games.

Osmani also said on his post, "We want Claude to be as helpful as possible when you're building something original, but also to protect your work if someone else tries to copy it."

Frontier AI is becoming increasingly powerful, allowing programmers and developers to get things done more quickly and efficiently. But its unprecedented capability is also leading to unintended consequences, such as the ability to create rough clones of expensive software, such as Adobe Creative Suite and Microsoft Office.

Amazon ditches GrubHub, taps Uber Eats for Prime

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

Amazon has made Uber Eats as its official restaurant delivery partner for Prime members in the United States, effectively ending the e-commerce giant’s existing deal with Grubhub. Under the new partnership announced Thursday, Amazon Prime members will be able to order food from Uber Eats directly through Amazon.com and the Amazon Shopping app. Importantly, Prime members won’t need to create an Uber account and won’t pay a delivery fee on orders of more than $25, the companies said.

The commercial agreement with Grubhub that gave Prime members access to Grubhub+ ends Thursday, according to an Amazon spokesperson, who said the company continuously evaluates its third-party offers. Amazon said that through that process it identified an opportunity to deliver an even better restaurant delivery experience.

The new partnership deepens an already extensive relationship between Amazon and Uber. The companies have existing agreements spanning cloud computing, Zoox robotaxis, and the integration of Uber Eats into Amazon’s AI-powered Alexa+ assistant.

It also allows Amazon to give its Prime members another sweetener to justify that annual membership fee. To help kickstart the initiative, Amazon and Uber are offering Prime members $20 off their first order of $25 or more during the first two weeks.

Amazon’s ouster of Grubhub ends a once-close relationship. Amazon first struck a deal with the company in 2022, offering Prime members a free Grubhub+ subscription.

Two years later, Amazon expanded its commercial agreement and made the free Grubhub+ membership an ongoing offer for Prime members. Amazon also reached a deal with Grubhub’s then-owner, Just Eat Takeaway.com, to increase its equity stake in the food delivery company as much as 18%.

Under that performance-based agreement, which was set to renew annually, Amazon could receive warrants representing an additional stake in Grubhub. But just months later, Just Eat Takeaway announced plans to sell Grubhub to Wonder. According to Just Eat Takeway’s annual report, Amazon elected in December 2024 to cash out on its outstanding exercisable warrants.

Amazon and Grubhub’s commercial partnership continued, however, until now.Grubhub could not be reached prior to publication. TechCrunch will update this article, if the company responds.

PS5 Exclusive Marvel's Wolverine Off to a Soft Start, 2.4 Million Copies Sold to Date

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Sony has officially confirmed sales figures for Marvel's Wolverine for the first time, as part of its recent financial update.

According to the report, the PS5 exclusive superhero action adventure has sold 2.4 million units as of 4th November, 2026.

The game launched on 15th September, so this sales figure is its performance after 50 days on the market.

While it may initially sound reasonably strong, it doesn't make for flattering comparisons with other titles.

For example, Marvel's Spider-Man 2 sold 2.5 million copies in its first 24 hours, and reached 5 million in 11 days.

Last year's Ghost of Yotei, meanwhile, reached 3.3 million sales in its first month.

With Marvel's Wolverine reportedly costing around $300 million to produce, it's seemingly still got some way to go before it's a profitable product.

Previous sales estimates from a couple of analytical firms have been proven fairly accurate, then, but it's always good to get proper, confirmed numbers from Sony itself.

While this sales figure means a fairly slow start for a game of this scope and budget, it's worth remembering that Marvel's Wolverine was the USA's top-selling game of September in full game dollar sales. It was also the best launch month for a Sony game since Helldivers 2.

Having said that, its early momentum doesn't appear to have carried through to October; in last month's PS Store charts, the game dropped to 14th in the US and isn't in Europe's top 20 at all.

So, it's been a success in some ways, but there's still some ground to make up. In a sense, Insomniac's Spider-Man series picks up the slack, having sold around 60 million units total, so I doubt Wolverine's somewhat soft sales performance will be too big a concern for the higher-ups.

Mixed news for this title, then, but overall I think it'll be fine — the game will slowly rack up more sales over time, and it's about to have its first holiday season, where there will no doubt be a spike.

Amazon teams up with Uber on restaurant delivery, years after shutting down its own service

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

by Kurt Schlosser on Nov 5, 2026 at 3:00 amNovember 4, 2026 at 1:39 pm

Amazon is bringing Uber Eats inside its own app, letting U.S. Prime members order restaurant delivery without a separate Uber account and skip the delivery fee on orders of $25 or more.

The deal replaces a Prime perk with Grubhub, which Amazon made an ongoing benefit in 2024 after a one-year trial. Amazon said in a statement to GeekWire that it has ended that agreement after identifying “an opportunity to deliver an even better restaurant delivery experience.”

The new Prime benefit covers hundreds of thousands of restaurants in nearly 10,000 U.S. cities and towns, from major metros to rural communities. Orders are placed with existing Amazon logins and payment methods, on Amazon.com or in the Amazon Shopping app.

Amazon says Prime members could save more than $175 a year through the delivery fee waiver and Prime-only promotions, which it notes is more than the $139 annual cost of Prime. Service fees still apply, the company confirmed. New users get $20 off their first two orders of $25 or more within 14 days, and Prime Visa cardholders earn 5% back on Uber Eats orders through Dec. 5.

Members who already pay for Uber One, which includes $0 delivery fees, keep those benefits. But Amazon said the two memberships operate independently, and the Prime perk applies only to Uber Eats orders placed through Amazon.

The deal marks Amazon’s latest approach to restaurant delivery. The company shut down its own Amazon Restaurants service in 2019, four years after launching it in Seattle and expanding to more than 20 U.S. cities. Uber had listed Amazon as an Uber Eats competitor in its IPO filing earlier that year.

The service let Prime members order meals through its website or the Prime Now app. Amazon cut jobs on the team in early 2018, closed the London service later that year, and also shut down Daily Dish, a workplace lunch delivery service it launched in 2016.

The Uber deal is part of Amazon’s broader push to add value to Prime. In February, the company made its Alexa+ AI assistant free for U.S. Prime members. Last year it ended a program that let members share free shipping with someone outside their household.

“We’re excited to connect our restaurant community with new Prime fans, while giving Prime members a seamless way to enjoy great food from restaurants through Uber Eats,” Andrew Macdonald, Uber’s president and COO, said in a statement.

Members can share the Uber Eats benefit with one adult in their household through Amazon Family.

Agentic AI Is Shipping Code. Who Owns The Risk?

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Andrea Scaduto is co-founder and CEO of SecureFlag, a secure coding training platform.

Not long ago, much of the discussion in software engineering circles was about whether developers should use AI to write code. Now, the focus is on agentic AI and agents that run commands with minimal human direction.

Gartner put agentic AI at the top of its 2026 cybersecurity trends, warning that the technology is creating new attack surfaces as employees and developers adopt it, and that no-code platforms and vibe coding are speeding up the spread of unmanaged agents, unsecured code and potential compliance violations.​​

Before AI agents, development gave organizations a fairly clear path to accountability. A developer makes the change, a reviewer checks it and the service team owns what reaches production.

The chain is now more complicated when an AI agent takes a feature request and turns it into code for a developer to review. For example, a product manager provides the instructions, the agent generates the change and the developer approves it. If that change exposes customer data, who owns the decision to ship it?

The most practical answer, I’d argue, is the person who approves the merge. Agent-written code becomes the organization’s code once a developer approves it, so that approval can’t be a formality. If the person signing off can’t explain what a change does and why it’s safe, it shouldn’t be deployed.

Customers won’t accept “the AI wrote it” as an explanation, and neither will auditors or regulators. The EU’s Cyber Resilience Act makes this clear by placing responsibility on the organizations that put products on the market.​

It’s certainly beneficial that teams can now produce more software faster, but they don’t automatically gain the capacity to assess it all. More output also means more changes to approve, and less time to give each one proper attention.

Research from The Consortium for Information & Software Quality estimated that the cost of poor software quality in the U.S. was approximately $2.41 trillion in 2022, with accumulated technical debt tracked separately at around $1.52 trillion. As teams produce more code, even a low rate of flawed code can create more technical debt than they can keep up with.

Some of that debt is also security debt, as security vulnerabilities found in production can become entry points for attackers and cost more to fix in the long run.​

​Any approach to governing agentic AI has to start with knowing where it is being used. Gartner’s research mentioned above recommends identifying both sanctioned and unsanctioned AI agents, establishing controls for each and preparing incident response plans for agent-related risks.

It’s harder than it sounds, as agents work in integrated development environment extensions, command-line tools, CI/CD pipelines and business-user platforms. Calling them all “AI coding assistants” can obscure how differently they operate. An agent that proposes a change for review has a very different level of access and autonomy from one that can modify a repository or deploy code. These differences should determine how each agent is governed.

For each agent, security and engineering leaders should know who uses it and what it is allowed to do. ​

Agents should have only the permissions they need, with human approval reserved for higher-risk actions, such as merging changes to protected branches or modifying authentication and sensitive data.

Routine changes can use the same security checks as human-authored code, with agent-generated changes clearly identified so teams can see where they came from. Some of these checks have limits, though. In my experience, scanners are effective at finding known patterns but much less reliable when a vulnerability depends on business logic or application context.

As co-founder and director of SecureFlag, a company that provides hands-on developer security training, I’m particularly interested in how developers build the skills to work securely with AI agents and review the code they produce. This focus informs my views on agentic AI, though I recognize that training is only one part of governing how these agents are used.​​​

It’s developers who need to find the issues scanners miss, but general awareness training doesn’t necessarily equip them to do so. Instead, Gartner recommends adaptive programs that include AI-specific tasks, and I’ve found hands-on practice in real development environments can help to build the skills to do this.​​

​Organizations can only take responsibility for agents if they know whether the controls around them are working and developers have the skills they need.

The most useful measures come from the code itself, such as comparing vulnerability rates in agent-authored and human-authored changes, and tracking that difference to see if risk goes down. Remediation time and recurring vulnerability classes can also show where problems continue to occur.

It’s also worth tracking how often teams catch problems in agent-authored changes before merge. A rising catch rate could mean controls are working better or agents are producing more problematic code, and it’s important to know which. These measures give a better view of how the organization is managing the risks of agentic development.​

Securing agentic development has two sides. The agent itself needs to be secured, with limits on what it can access and change. The software it produces also needs to be secure, which depends on someone understanding what the agent was asked to do and whether the result matches that intent.

Developers are at the center of both. In many organizations today, agents operate with some of the same access as the developer using them, meaning developers often influence what agents can access without making a deliberate choice. They are also accountable for the changes they approve.

Leadership is responsible for the conditions around that work, including setting boundaries for what agents can access and making sure developers have the skills to configure agents securely and review what they produce. The organization carries the risk either way.

As agents take on more of the development process, organizations need to define where human responsibility lies. Waiting for an incident to establish those boundaries is a costly way to find out.​
